If $a$ is constant, the stopping case in [thm:interpolation](#thm:interpolation) applies directly.

now gives $h\le C_3d^{C_4}$ for the selected lifts. Its constants are uniform because the cover, centers, allowed ordinary systems, and isogeny multipliers are fixed.

*Descent from the selected lifts.* The degree of the marking cover is fixed. If $t_*$ is the image of $t$, then $$[K(t):K]\le (\deg(S\to S_*))[K(t_*):K].$$ Choose the ample height upstairs as the height of the pullback of an ample divisor downstairs; it equals the downstairs height up to a bounded error, and arbitrary fixed ample heights on these curves are comparable up to fixed positive constants and bounded errors. Thus the bound for the selected lift descends to $t_*$. The further normalization and fine-level maps descend in the same way by [lem:finite-covers](#lem:finite-covers). For any other lift $\widetilde t$ in a fixed cover, height comparison and $[K(t_*):K]\le[K(\widetilde t):K]$ give the same numerical bound. Thus only the selected lifts require <a href="#eq:marked-incidence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marked-incidence">[eq:marked-incidence]</a>. At singular coarse points there are finitely many normalization preimages, all included among the finite exceptions. Increasing the constants includes all finitely many omitted points. The absolute height bound for a selected lift also holds for all its conjugates over the fixed data field, even when those conjugates were not selected relative to the original marking of $t_0$; no fresh incidence choice is needed to deduce that numerical bound. All fixed enlargements of the data field $K$ change the degree bounds by at most fixed factors. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:counting"></a>
### From heights to finiteness

We now turn the height estimate into a bound on degrees. The rational objects to be counted are ordered pairs of integral Hodge vectors in the rank-five variation. Their two orthogonality equations cut the period quadric in a conic. A semialgebraic family of such pairs with one real parameter therefore sweeps out an algebraic subset of dimension at most two, whereas full monodromy forces the period image to be Zariski dense in the three-dimensional quadric.

Throughout this section the curve, family, and all auxiliary covers are fixed over a number field $K$. We choose the ample height on $\bar S$ from a fixed projective embedding defined over $K$, and write $$h(t)=\max\{2,h_{\bar S}(t)\},\qquad
 d(t)=\max\{2,[K(t):K]\}.$$ This choice is invariant under $\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb Q}/K)$; changing to it preserves the preceding estimates by the height comparisons in [lem:finite-covers](#lem:finite-covers). Constants in this section depend on the fixed data.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Flat coordinates at all complex embeddings

The Rosati bound in [prop:small-vectors](#prop:small-vectors) controls the Hodge norms of our vectors. Counting requires their numerical sizes in integral flat bases. Near a puncture, passing between these two bounds also requires control of the dual Hodge norm at each complex embedding.

Write $b=\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle$ for the trace pairing of <a href="#eq:quadratic-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-system">[eq:quadratic-system]</a>. In a flat trivialization of $\mathbb V_{\mathbb Z}$, let $V_{\mathbb Z}$ be the corresponding fixed lattice and $V_{\mathbb C}=V_{\mathbb Z}\otimes\mathbb C$. The period quadric of <a href="#eq:period-quadric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:period-quadric">[eq:period-quadric]</a> is $$Q_V=\{[w]\in\mathbb P(V_{\mathbb C}):b(w,w)=0\},
 \qquad \dim_{\mathbb C}Q_V=3.$$ The period line is the line corresponding to $H^{2,0}$, with the Tate twist understood. A real vector of type $(0,0)$ is orthogonal to this line. In particular the two vectors of [prop:small-vectors](#prop:small-vectors) satisfy these equations, and their Gram matrix for $b$ is positive definite by [lem:qm-plane](#lem:qm-plane).

Here the counting height is the *multiplicative rational coordinate height*. For $y=(y_1,\ldots,y_m)\in\mathbb Q^m$, with $y_i=a_i/b_i$ in lowest terms and $b_i>0$, put $$H_{\mathrm{rat}}(y)=\max_i\max\{\left\lvert a_i\right\rvert,b_i\}.$$ For an integral tuple it is $\max\{1,\left\lVert y\right\rVert_{\infty}\}$. This height is distinct from the absolute logarithmic algebraic heights used in the arithmetic argument.

<a id="stmt-lem-flat-coordinate-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:flat-coordinate-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:flat-coordinate-height"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Flat-coordinate bound). *There is a finite collection of simply connected complex charts covering $S(\mathbb C)$, each equipped with an integral flat basis of $\mathbb V_{\mathbb Z}$, whose period maps are definable in $\mathbb R_{\mathrm{an},\exp}$. There are constants $c,M>0$ such that, for every QM point $t\in S(\overline{\mathbb Q})$, the ordered pair of integral vectors supplied by [prop:small-vectors](#prop:small-vectors) has flat coordinate tuple $y\in\mathbb Z^{10}$ satisfying $$\label{eq:flat-coordinate-height}
 H_{\mathrm{rat}}(y)\le c\bigl(d(t)h(t)\bigr)^M$$ in each of these charts containing $t$. The same bound holds for their conjugate vectors at every conjugate of $t$ over $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-flat-coordinate-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-flat-coordinate-bound</summary>

*Proof.* We first describe the charts and their metric estimates, including the punctures of $S$. A compact subset away from the finitely many points of $\bar S\setminus S$ has a finite cover by relatively compact simply connected coordinate disks. Shrink these disks inside slightly larger disks on which the flat trivializations and the period maps are holomorphic. Their real and imaginary parts are restricted analytic functions, hence definable.

<a id="eq:sector-period"></a>
At a puncture choose a rational function $u$ on $\bar S$ which is a local parameter there. Enlarge $K$ once to define these finitely many choices. The local monodromy is quasi-unipotent. On a fixed local cover $u=w^e$ it is unipotent, say $\exp N$, with $N$ nilpotent. The one-variable nilpotent orbit theorem (Schmid 1973, Theorem 4.9) <a id="pm-cite-60"></a>[[Schmid1973](#ref-Schmid1973)] gives, in the compact dual, the expression $$\label{eq:sector-period}
 \Phi(u)=
 \exp\left(\frac{\log w}{2\pi i}N\right)\Psi(w),$$ where $\Psi$ extends holomorphically across $w=0$. Cover a sufficiently small punctured $u$-disk by finitely many open angular sectors of width less than $2\pi$, and choose a branch of $w$ and of $\log w$ on each. The exponential in <a href="#eq:sector-period" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-period">[eq:sector-period]</a> is a polynomial in $\log w$. The branch of $w$ is algebraic, its argument is bounded, and $\log\left\lvert w\right\rvert$ is definable in $\mathbb R_{\mathrm{an},\exp}$. After shrinking the radius, $\Psi$ is restricted analytic in an affine chart about $\Psi(0)$. Thus the projective map <a href="#eq:sector-period" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-period">[eq:sector-period]</a> is definable. These local covers are used only to describe functions on the sectors; no point-dependent global cover is introduced.

<a id="eq:sector-metric-dual"></a>
Write $\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{H,u}$ for the Hodge norm. Schmid’s abstract one-variable norm estimate (Schmid 1973, Theorem 6.6$'$) <a id="pm-cite-61"></a>[[Schmid1973](#ref-Schmid1973)], applied both to $\mathbb V$ and to its dual, bounds the norms of each fixed flat basis and dual basis by a power of $1+\log(1/\left\lvert u\right\rvert)$, uniformly on each sector. Indeed every fixed flat vector belongs to some step of the monodromy weight filtration, and that theorem supplies such a power; only finitely many basis vectors occur. Passing from $w$ to $u=w^e$ merely changes the constants. Consequently, with $e_1,\ldots,e_5$ an integral flat basis and $e_1^*,\ldots,e_5^*$ its dual, we have fixed $c_0,r>0$ such that $$\label{eq:sector-metric-dual}
 \max_i\bigl\{\left\lVert e_i\right\rVert_{H,u},
                  \left\lVert e_i^*\right\rVert_{H,u}^{\vee}\bigr\}
 \le c_0\bigl(1+\log(1/\left\lvert u\right\rvert)\bigr)^r.$$ On the compact charts the corresponding bound is constant. In particular <a href="#eq:sector-metric-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-metric-dual">[eq:sector-metric-dual]</a> controls the metric and its inverse in flat coordinates.

<a id="eq:individual-embedding-log"></a>
For completeness, the logarithm in this bound can be controlled at an individual complex embedding, not merely on average. Let $F=K(t)$ and let $\sigma:F\hookrightarrow\mathbb C$ fix the chosen embedding of $K$. Whenever $\sigma(t)$ belongs to the sector, $u(t)\ne0$, and the absolute height identity $\mathrm h\!\left(u(t)^{-1}\right)=\mathrm h\!\left(u(t)\right)$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \log^+\frac1{\left\lvert\sigma(u(t))\right\rvert}
 &\le [F:\mathbb Q]\mathrm h\!\left(u(t)\right)\notag\\
 &\le c_1d(t)h(t).\label{eq:individual-embedding-log}
\end{aligned}$$ The first inequality follows by retaining that archimedean summand in the height of $u(t)^{-1}$, using the usual nonsquared absolute value. For the second, the fixed rational function $u$ defines a morphism $\bar S\to\mathbb P^1$, whose height is bounded above by a constant times $1+h_{\bar S}$. This also explains why algebraic local parameters were chosen.

Let $v$ be either of the two chosen endomorphism vectors, at any such embedding. If $v$ realizes the symmetric endomorphism $\alpha$, the Weil operator fixes its type $(0,0)$, and its rationality gives $$\left\lVert v\right\rVert_H^2=b(v,v)=\tfrac14\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\alpha^2)
              =\tfrac14 q_{\lambda_t}(\alpha).$$ The bound in [prop:small-vectors](#prop:small-vectors) therefore gives $\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H}\le c_2(d(t)h(t))^{r_2}$. If $v=\sum_i n_i e_i$, the dual norm inequality is $$\left\lvert n_i\right\rvert=\left\lvert e_i^*(v)\right\rvert
 \le\left\lVert e_i^*\right\rVert_{H}^{\vee}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H}.$$ Combining this with <a href="#eq:sector-metric-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-metric-dual">[eq:sector-metric-dual]</a> and <a href="#eq:individual-embedding-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:individual-embedding-log">[eq:individual-embedding-log]</a> proves a polynomial bound on the *numerical sizes* of all ten integers. This is precisely <a href="#eq:flat-coordinate-height" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-coordinate-height">[eq:flat-coordinate-height]</a>.

The vectors are realizations of actual integral endomorphisms. Conjugation preserves integrality, independence, and the rational Rosati trace norms. Although labeling the endomorphisms may require an extension of $K(t)$ of bounded relative degree, each embedding of $K(t)$ extends to that field. Conjugating the labeled pair along any such extension supplies the asserted pair at the conjugate point. Neither the degree estimate for $u(t)$ nor the number of conjugate points is diminished by this labeling extension. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Counting distinct curve arguments

We use the following precise specialization of the counting theorem. The structure $\mathbb R_{\mathrm{an},\exp}$ containing our period charts is o-minimal by (Dries, Macintyre, and Marker 1994, Corollary 5.13) <a id="pm-cite-62"></a>[[DMM1994](#ref-DMM1994)]. Let $Z\subset\mathbb R^m\times\mathbb R^n$ be definable in an o-minimal expansion of the real field, and write $\pi_1,\pi_2$ for its two projections. For every $\eta>0$ there is $c_Z(\eta)$ such that, if $T\ge1$ and $$\Sigma\subset
 \{(y,z)\in Z:y\in\mathbb Q^m,\ H_{\mathrm{rat}}(y)\le T\},
 \qquad \#\pi_2(\Sigma)>c_Z(\eta)T^\eta,$$ then there is a continuous definable path $\beta:[0,1]\to Z$ for which $\pi_1\beta$ is semialgebraic and real analytic on $(0,1)$, while $\pi_2\beta$ is nonconstant. This is (Habegger and Pila 2016, Corollary 7.2, with $k=1$ and no family parameter) <a id="pm-cite-63"></a>[[HabeggerPila2016](#ref-HabeggerPila2016)]. The corollary counts distinct second projections and imposes no finite-fiber hypothesis on $Z\to\mathbb R^m$. A constant first projection is allowed, and causes no difficulty below.

<a id="stmt-prop-counting-finiteness"></a>
<div id="prop:counting-finiteness" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:counting-height-hypothesis"></a>
**Proposition 22**. *For fixed $A,\kappa>0$, the set of QM points $t\in S(\overline{\mathbb Q})$ satisfying $$\label{eq:counting-height-hypothesis}
 h(t)\le A d(t)^\kappa$$ is finite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-counting-finiteness"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-counting-finiteness</summary>

<a id="eq:counting-polynomial-T"></a>
*Proof.* The set in question is stable under $\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb Q}/K)$. If its degrees were unbounded, take a point $t$ of sufficiently large degree $d=[K(t):K]$. Its full orbit has $d$ distinct points on $S(\mathbb C)$, all satisfying <a href="#eq:counting-height-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:counting-height-hypothesis">[eq:counting-height-hypothesis]</a>. Assign each conjugate to one chart of [lem:flat-coordinate-bound](#lem:flat-coordinate-bound). If there are $J$ charts, one chart contains at least $d/J$ of these distinct points. For each of them choose a conjugate of the labeled pair at $t$. Their integral coordinate tuples have $$\label{eq:counting-polynomial-T}
 H_{\mathrm{rat}}(y)\le T=c_3d^B$$ for fixed $c_3,B>0$.

<a id="eq:counting-incidence"></a>
In the selected chart write $z\in D\subset\mathbb R^2$ for the curve coordinate and $\Phi(z)\in Q_V$ for the period line. Identify the flat real space with $\mathbb R^5$. Consider the fixed definable set $$\label{eq:counting-incidence}
 Z=\left\{((v_1,v_2),z)\in(\mathbb R^5)^2\times D:
 \begin{array}{l}
   (b(v_i,v_j))_{i,j=1}^2\text{ is positive definite},\\
   b(v_1,\Phi(z))=b(v_2,\Phi(z))=0
 \end{array}\right\}.$$ Orthogonality to a projective line means orthogonality to any nonzero representative; it is a well-defined algebraic incidence condition. The coordinate $z$ is injective on the chart. The tuples just chosen thus form a set $\Sigma\subset Z$ with at least $d/J$ distinct second projections and rational first coordinates bounded as in <a href="#eq:counting-polynomial-T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:counting-polynomial-T">[eq:counting-polynomial-T]</a>.

Choose $\eta>0$ with $B\eta<1$. The finitely many chart incidences have a common counting constant, obtained by taking their maximum. For large enough $d$ we have $$d/J>c_Z(\eta)(c_3d^B)^\eta.$$ The quoted corollary therefore gives a path $\beta(s)=(\gamma(s),z(s))$ in $Z$, with $\gamma$ semialgebraic and $z$ nonconstant. It remains to show that this path would force the period image into a proper algebraic subset of $Q_V$. The first projection supplies at most one algebraic parameter, and for each nondegenerate pair the orthogonal period lines form a conic.

Let $\mathcal A\subset(\mathbb C^5)^2$ be the complex Zariski closure of $\gamma((0,1))$. A semialgebraic set of real dimension at most one has real Zariski closure of dimension at most one, and complexification preserves that dimension. Hence $\dim_{\mathbb C}\mathcal A\le1$. Equivalently, after a finite semialgebraic decomposition, the arc has Nash coordinate functions, which are algebraic over its real parameter; their algebraic closure has transcendence degree at most one.

<a id="eq:counting-dimension-two"></a>
Crucially, impose nondegeneracy before closing the orthogonality incidence. Let $$\mathcal U=
 \{(v_1,v_2):\det(b(v_i,v_j))_{i,j=1}^2\ne0\}
 \subset(\mathbb C^5)^2,$$ and define the locally closed algebraic set $$\mathcal I=
 \{(v_1,v_2,[w])\in(\mathcal A\cap\mathcal U)\times Q_V:
       b(v_1,w)=b(v_2,w)=0\}.$$ For each $(v_1,v_2)\in\mathcal A\cap\mathcal U$, their span is a nondegenerate two-space. Its perpendicular is a nondegenerate three-space, whose isotropic lines form a smooth projective conic. Every fiber of $\mathcal I\to\mathcal A\cap\mathcal U$ therefore has dimension one, so $$\dim_{\mathbb C}\mathcal I\le2.$$ Let $X\subset Q_V$ be the Zariski closure of its image under the second projection. The dimension of an image and its closure cannot exceed the dimension of its source, whence $$\label{eq:counting-dimension-two}
 \dim_{\mathbb C}X\le2.$$ This construction closes only the incidence over $\mathcal A\cap\mathcal U$; it does not add arbitrary fibers over degenerate pairs. Since the pairs on $\gamma$ have positive definite Gram matrix, they lie in $\mathcal U$, and consequently $\Phi(z(s))\in X$ for all $s\in(0,1)$.

The continuous nonconstant path $z(s)$ contains infinitely many distinct points accumulating inside the chart. Indeed one may restrict it to a closed interior interval on which it remains nonconstant. The inverse image $\Phi^{-1}(X)$ is a complex analytic subset of a connected one-dimensional chart. The identity theorem therefore makes it the entire chart. Continuing the defining homogeneous polynomial identities along $S$ gives $$\widetilde\Phi(\widetilde S)\subset X,$$ where $\widetilde S$ is the connected universal cover and the flat trivialization defining $\widetilde\Phi$ extends the one in the chosen chart.

Fix a period line $L$ in this image. Equivariance places its entire monodromy orbit $\Gamma L$ in $X$. By [prop:monodromy](#prop:monodromy), $\Gamma$ is Zariski dense in $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(V_{\mathbb C},b)$, and this group acts transitively on the nonsingular isotropic quadric $Q_V$. The orbit $\Gamma L$ is therefore Zariski dense in $Q_V$. This contradicts <a href="#eq:counting-dimension-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:counting-dimension-two">[eq:counting-dimension-two]</a> and $\dim Q_V=3$.

Degrees in <a href="#eq:counting-height-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:counting-height-hypothesis">[eq:counting-height-hypothesis]</a> are thus bounded. That inequality also bounds their ample heights, and Northcott’s theorem on the fixed projective curve $\bar S$ proves finiteness. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Descent to the coarse curve

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-counting-finiteness-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-counting-finiteness</summary>

*Proof of [thm:main](#thm:main).* Suppose the stated locus on $C$ were infinite. By [lem:finite-covers](#lem:finite-covers), normalize $C$ and take a connected component of a fixed fine-level cover containing infinitely many lifted target points. Removing the finitely many points needed for the smooth family and the algebraic charts leaves infinitely many. Choose an interior QM center and then the fixed auxiliary cover of [prop:marking](#prop:marking). All these choices, including their fields of definition, are made once.

For every remaining target to which the construction is applied, [thm:height](#thm:height) supplies a selected lift on this fixed cover with $h(t)\le A d(t)^\kappa$, for fixed $A,\kappa$. The degrees of a point and its lift are comparable by the fixed degree of the finite map, and the ample heights are comparable by the pullback of ample divisors, as in [lem:finite-covers](#lem:finite-covers). Thus the constants are uniform as the quaternion algebra and the target vary.

Apply [prop:counting-finiteness](#prop:counting-finiteness) on the fixed covering curve. Its counting argument uses the full orbit over the fixed field $K$: the height inequality for a selected lift is invariant under that orbit, and conjugation preserves its two integral Hodge vectors and their Rosati bounds. A conjugate marking need not satisfy the incidence imposed relative to the chosen center. That incidence was used to prove the height inequality; the incidence <a href="#eq:counting-incidence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:counting-incidence">[eq:counting-incidence]</a> depends only on the target’s Hodge vectors. Hence all these selected lifts belong to the finite set of [prop:counting-finiteness](#prop:counting-finiteness), whose final step uses Northcott’s theorem. Their images on $C$ form a finite set. Adding back the finitely many removed points contradicts the assumed infinitude. This proves the asserted finiteness for full geometric endomorphism algebra an indefinite quaternion division algebra over $\mathbb Q$, with no restriction on its discriminant or order. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### References
